# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-11

## RAG
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[SNAPRAG: Cost-Efficient Knowledge Extraction Attack on RAG Systems via Adversarial Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12241v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems improve Large Language Model (LLM) responses by incorporating information from external knowledge bases. However, recent studies have shown that attackers can extract substantial portions of private knowledge bases from RAG systems through repeated querying. Yet, state-of-the-art extraction attacks either rely heavily on external LLMs to generate adversarial queries or require a large number of queries. In this paper, we propose SNAPRAG, a novel and cost-efficient attack for extracting RAG knowledge bases through adversarial perturbations. Our approach algorithmically generates adversarial queries without relying on external LLMs and allows for a strictly configurable query budget. In particular, SNAPRAG uses a discrete token-mutation algorithm to generate novel queries in a strict black-box setting. Our adaptive dissimilarity threshold calibration systematically forces queries into unexplored semantic regions of the target database, enabling extraction with fewer queries. Specifically, to extract half of the target knowledge base, our method requires up to 6.77$\times$ fewer queries than existing approaches. Furthermore, within a fixed query budget, our method recovers up to 96.9%, outperforming the closest approach by 7.7-19.9%. We further evaluate SNAPRAG against active defenses, where it remains the leading attack on four of five defended targets, achieving 50.7-96.3% extraction rate.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 6 figures, including appendices</p></details> |
| **[ARMOR: Adaptive Curriculum Meta-Learning for Noise-Robust RAG Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11771v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems have demonstrated remarkable effectiveness in mitigating hallucinations by incorporating external knowledge. However, the retrieval process inevitably introduces noise, posing significant challenges to RAG robustness. Fundamentally, noise robustness is a composite competency encompassing noise identification, retrieval filtering, and robust reasoning. Unfortunately, existing methods often fail to capture this holistic nature, typically restricted to single-dimensional enhancements or hampered by noise-induced optimization interference. To address these limitations, we decompose RAG robustness into three sub-tasks: knowledge boundary perception, retrieval verification, and robust reasoning. Accordingly, we propose ARMOR, an Adaptive Curriculum Meta-Learning for Noise Robust RAG Reasoning. Inspired by the teaching process, ARMOR introduces an adaptive task scheduler as a teacher to curate training plans according to the absolute learning progress of RAG generators (Student). Distinct from GRPO, ARMOR utilizes inter-group advantage estimation during learning to effectively mitigate mode collapse caused by limited output spaces. Experiments on three complex reasoning datasets (2WikiMultiHopQA, MusiQue, and HotPotQA) indicate that ARMOR achieves synergistic improvements in retrieval noise robustness, significantly outperforming baselines in output quality.</p></details> |  |
| **[PCG-RAG: Progressive Compositional Grounding for Retrieval-Augmented Generation in Complex Web Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11113v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Complex Web question answering requires retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems to identify target entities from multi-clue descriptions and connect evidence scattered across long Web pages. Individual pages often support only some of the required semantic constraints, while clues discovered on one page guide subsequent searches on others. However, premature reliance on partially supported entity hypotheses can divert retrieval from the original information need. RAG systems must therefore use partial evidence to guide further searches while seeking the evidence needed to establish the intended entities. We propose PCG-RAG, a novel RAG framework based on Progressive Compositional Grounding (PCG). Progressive Evidence Acquisition directs subsequent searches toward missing evidence for candidate attributes and relations. Compositional Reasoning separates candidate-guided retrieval from evidence-based answering along a directed acyclic graph (DAG) of subquestions. Provisional names make dependent searches specific, while answering tasks use accepted results and unresolved identifying conditions, assessed against the original question. New passages connect the identifying clues to the requested relations, supporting evidence-based downstream answers. Evidence Grounding combines identifying and relational source passages with supported intermediate answers, connecting evidence across Web pages to the original conditions during final generation. On BrowseComp-Plus and WebWalkerQA, PCG-RAG improves accuracy over the leading compared baselines by 5.43 and 5.15 percentage points, respectively.</p></details> |  |
| **[MMGraph-Agent: Agentic Multimodal RAG via Cache-Inspired Multimodal Knowledge HyperGraphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10657v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Driven by increasingly complex real-world applications, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has evolved from textual pipelines to multimodal settings, comprising two complementary dimensions: knowledge organization and retrieval enhancement. However, existing methods still face challenges of high construction cost, context window limitations, and the lack of unified training across offline and online knowledge sources. To address these challenges, we propose MMGraph-Agent, a unified multimodal RAG framework based on cache-inspired multimodal knowledge hypergraphs. MMGraph-Agent enables extremely lightweight hypergraph construction with zero API cost and dynamic memory-based retrieval that alleviates long-context issues. Experiments across offline, online, and joint retrieval settings demonstrate consistent improvements in performance, efficiency, and architectural unification. Our software and data are publicly available at https://github.com/LHRLAB/MMGraph-Agent.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026 main conference</p></details> |
| **[RAG vs. GraphRAG: A Systematic Evaluation and Key Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.11371v4)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) improves large language models (LLMs) by retrieving relevant information from external sources and has been widely adopted for text-based tasks. For structured data, such as knowledge graphs, Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) retrieves and aggregates information along graph structures. More recently, GraphRAG has been extended to general text settings by organizing unstructured text into graph representations, showing promise for reasoning and grounding. Despite these advances, existing GraphRAG systems for text data are often tailored to specific tasks, datasets, and system designs, resulting in heterogeneous evaluation protocols. Consequently, a systematic understanding of the relative strengths, limitations, and trade-offs between RAG and GraphRAG on widely used text benchmarks remains limited. In this paper, we present a comprehensive benchmark study comparing RAG and GraphRAG on established text-based tasks, including question answering and query-based summarization. We introduce a unified evaluation protocol that standardizes data preprocessing, retrieval configurations, and generation settings, enabling fair and reproducible comparisons. Our results highlight the distinct strengths of RAG and GraphRAG across different tasks and evaluation perspectives. Building on these findings, we explore selection and integration strategies that combine the strengths of both paradigms, leading to consistent performance improvements. We further analyze failure modes, efficiency trade-offs, and evaluation biases, and highlight key considerations for designing and evaluating retrieval-augmented generation systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[PatternBloom: Empowering Agentic RAG with Externalized RL-Distilled Graph Patterns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10477v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement-learning-trained agents for retrieval-augmented generation retrieve content (passages, knowledge-graph fragments, or hyperedges) and supervise training with outcome rewards on the final answer. Across these paradigms the reasoning route through retrieved content never leaves the language model's hidden state, conflating declarative knowledge with the procedural knowledge that recurs across queries of the same logical type; content-only methods consequently plateau as reasoning depth grows. We introduce PatternBloom, an agentic RAG framework that shifts the unit of retrieval from content (what to read) to logic (how to reason). A first RL stage scores the agent's constructed evidence graph by a frozen oracle's information gain through the Information-Density Reward (IDR), a size-normalized mutual-information surrogate. High-reward trajectories are distilled into a Graph Pattern Memory (GPM) of type-abstracted reasoning skeletons, and a second RL stage uses the Pattern-Augmented Reward (PAR) to couple policy to memory, turning procedural reasoning into a learnable, externalized structural prior. A 7B PatternBloom outperforms $27$ baselines by $+15.0$ Avg-OOD EM, with the gain compounding with reasoning depth precisely because procedural knowledge, once distilled, is reused rather than re-derived at every query. We release our code at https://github.com/EllenYiGe/PatternBloom.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. 46 pages. Code: https://github.com/EllenYiGe/PatternBloom</p></details> |
| **[Towards an Autonomous AI Steward for Large-Scale Physics Experiments: Integrating Causal Diagnostics, RAG, and Conformal Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09298v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-term particle physics experiments face critical operational challenges: monitoring non-stationary machine data streams and preserving tacit expert knowledge. We present the "Helmholtz Co-Pilot," a hybrid Multi-Agent System (MAS) acting as an autonomous experiment steward. The system can be triggered interactively by human operators or autonomously by an asynchronous causal diagnostics engine that runs independently of the MAS reasoning core. Continuously monitoring live hardware streams such as data acquisition and slow control, this engine performs dynamic causal discovery to trace cascading alarms back to their root causes and applies adaptive model selection to keep forecasts reliable across shifting machine states; upon detecting an anomaly, it hands a structured diagnostic report to the MAS. The reasoning layer itself relies on a high-throughput C++ core and a Python edge architecture orchestrating a directed acyclic graph (DAG) of specialized ReAct workers. It integrates a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) backbone for institutional memory and leverages conformal prediction to bound its evidence-based recommendations with mathematical coverage guarantees. This extended abstract outlines the architecture, the integration of software-enforced Human-in-the-Loop (HitL) guardrails, and ongoing shadow-testing within the KATRIN experiment.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages, 1 figure, 2nd International Conference on AI for Science (AI4Sci2026), 5-9 October 2026, Mainz, Germany (PoS)</p></details> |
| **[SkinMind: Equipping Agentic RAG with Human-Inspired Graph Probing for Reliable Dermatology AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08935v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable dermatology AI requires access to verifiable evidence distributed across heterogeneous textual and visual sources. However, conventional retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) typically relies on one-shot retrieval from flat corpora, limiting its ability to adapt retrieval granularity, explore multi-hop relations, and integrate multimodal clinical evidence. We introduce SkinMind, a multimodal agentic RAG framework that equips policy models with human-inspired graph probing for autonomous evidence seeking. SkinMind organizes retrieval as an iterative interaction between an orchestrator and a graph environment. Through eight hierarchical graph-probing tools, the orchestrator progressively identifies relevant entities, inspects node-level information, traverses relations and paths, and retrieves source-text and visual evidence. This progressive-disclosure design allows the model to adaptively determine the retrieval modality, exploration direction, and evidence granularity while constructing a query-specific evidence subgraph. To support autonomous graph probing, we construct SkinMind-KG from a 62M-token corpus of dermatology guidelines and textbooks and 423K images, guided by a dermatologist-informed schema and a conflict-resolution mechanism. The resulting graph contains over 1.0M nodes and 2.0M edges, unifying textual, relational, and visual evidence for retrieval. Across six dermatology VQA benchmarks and two policy-model settings, SkinMind improves the average score over direct answering by 8.70 and 8.89 points with GPT-5.4-mini and Gemini-2.5-Flash, respectively, while consistently outperforming competing RAG methods. Together, SkinMind and SkinMind-KG provide an effective foundation for evidence-grounded multimodal reasoning in dermatology.</p></details> | 35 pages, 7 figures |
| **[Who Speaks Matters: Authority-Aware Multi-View RAG over Italian Parliamentary Proceedings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13410v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Parliamentary proceedings are a primary record of democratic deliberation, yet their volume and fragmentation make multi-perspective access difficult for citizens, journalists, and researchers. Applying Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to parliamentary transcripts introduces three specific risks: dominance of the most frequent speakers, inability to weight speakers according to topical expertise, and citation misattribution in politically sensitive text. We present ParliamentRAG, a RAG system for the Italian Chamber of Deputies that addresses these risks jointly. Its core contribution is a topic-dependent authority model that estimates each speaker's authority as a function of the current query, combining interpretable components such as profession, education, and previous interventions. Given a user query, the system retrieves relevant speech chunks, identifies topic-relevant experts across parliamentary groups, and generates a summary synthesizing their perspectives, accompanied by supporting quotations. ParliamentRAG is evaluated against Google NotebookLM on 15 policy topics via a two-level protocol combining automated metrics and blind A/B human evaluation by six domain experts. The system achieves higher coverage across political groups (0.97 vs. 0.95), perfect quotation faithfulness (1.00 vs. 0.95), and stronger expert preferences on source-related dimensions, while NotebookLM remains stronger on prose-oriented dimensions.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at ISWC 2026 In-Use Track. Please cite the ISWC version</p></details> |
| **[Multimodal Graph RAG for Long-range Visually Rich Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28780v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are widely applied to visual document understanding. However, comprehending long documents remains an issue by the limited context window. Though recent multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (MMRAG) can address this challenge by retrieving relevant pages. It still struggles with the visual question answering (VQA) requiring holistic comprehension of a document. To cope with this, knowledge graph (KG) that summarizes global knowledge of a document can provide an effective solution. However, most existing LLM-based KG construction methods handle only the language modality, leaving the automatic creation of multimodal KGs (MMKGs) for visually rich documents largely unexplored. In this paper, we introduce a multimodal graph-based RAG approach to tackle this problem. Existing LLM-based KG methods evaluate the QA performance relying on indirect evidence such as comprehensiveness, diversity, empowerment, and so on. The lack of annotated datasets for comprehensive document-level VQA poses a significant challenge to effective model evaluation. To overcome this limitation, we also introduce a new benchmark, DLVQA (document-level VQA), which provides reference summaries and corresponding supporting facts for global document-level questions. Experimental results show that our approach outperforms existing MMRAG or KG-based approaches on multi-hop QA/VQA benchmarks and DLVQA.</p></details> | WACV 2027 |
| **[HyperGraphPro: Progress-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Structure-Guided Hypergraph RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17755v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-hop question answering requires retrieval agents to construct a connected evidence path across reasoning turns rather than retrieve isolated relevant evidence for a single query. However, in existing GraphRAG methods, retrieval and reinforcement learning are often optimized with mismatched signals: retrieval is driven by local semantic relevance, while outcome-level reinforcement learning assigns the reward by assessing the final answer without identifying which retrieval actions progress the reasoning path. We introduce HyperGraphPro, a progress-aware hypergraph RAG framework that focuses on evidence-path progress as the shared objective for retrieval and policy optimization. For retrieval, HyperGraphPro reranks candidate hyperedges using query-conditioned structural distinctiveness, combining entity-query relevance with a local-vs-global hypergraph incidence signal to favor entities that are informative in the current graph neighborhood. For policy optimization, HyperGraphPro augments uniform trajectory-level reward assignment with stepwise progress-aware policy optimization, which assigns step-level rewards according to the coverage of answer-guided target hyperedges. Experiments on multi-hop question answering benchmarks demonstrate that HyperGraphPro consistently improves reasoning accuracy over existing GraphRAG methods.</p></details> | In progress |
| **[Beyond Visual Boundaries: Rethinking Scene Segmentation for Movie RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28699v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Understanding long-form video remains a fundamental challenge for multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Sparse frame sampling fails to capture fine-grained visual details, while dense sampling quickly exceeds context length limits. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) offers a promising middle ground by selectively retrieving relevant video segments for grounded generation, yet its effectiveness critically depends on the quality of the video segments used as retrieval units. In this paper, we investigate RAG for movie understanding, which demands story-level reasoning over characters, events, and narrative arcs spanning hours of content. Scene segmentation, a long-studied problem that partitions movies into semantically coherent units, is a natural candidate for defining such retrieval units. We reexamine whether existing methods actually serve this role through comprehensive evaluation on downstream movie understanding tasks, and find that they do not provide a consistent or dominant advantage over naive uniform temporal chunking. Our audit of the most standard scene segmentation benchmarks reveals why: current annotations prioritize visually salient transitions over narrative event structure. Motivated by this mismatch, we introduce NarraScene, a narrative-centric scene segmentation dataset annotated with a three-level cognitive taxonomy spanning physical, character, and narrative change, where every valid boundary requires a narrative-level shift. When used as retrieval units, these narrative-grounded segments outperform uniform chunking on downstream movie understanding tasks, suggesting that the central challenge for scene segmentation in movie RAG is not detecting boundaries, but identifying the narrative event units that matter for movie understanding. The dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/queez0405/NarraScene.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations & Datasets Track)</p></details> |
| **[Embodied RAG++: Retrieval-Augmented Generation From What Exists to What Works in Embodied Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07517v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To serve a request in a human environment, a robot system must find an object that satisfies a functional requirement and a robot that can feasibly reach it. Scene graphs record which objects exist and where, but they often omit capabilities documented only in product manuals. Retrieval-augmented generation methods can read such manuals, yet they rarely reason jointly over object capabilities, physical instances, robot states, and dynamic connectivity. We introduce E2W-Bench, the Exists-to-Works Benchmark, which links dynamic hybrid scene graphs with real product manuals to evaluate capability-grounded robot--object assignment under availability, mobility, vertical-reach, and connector constraints. We also present Embodied RAG++, a spatially grounded retrieval framework that scopes retrieval to the manuals linked to candidate objects, caches extracted capabilities per product model, and resolves robot--object feasibility through deterministic spatial reasoning. On E2W-Bench, Embodied RAG++ reaches a pair accuracy of 0.90 with Qwen2.5-32B and 1.00 with Llama3.3-70B, compared with at most 0.30 and 0.55 for the baselines, while requiring the shortest preprocessing time among the retrieval-based methods and tying for the lowest total token count.</p></details> | accepted to SII 2027 |
| **[Trustworthy RAG: An Evaluation Agent for Detecting Misinformation and Knowledge Poisoning in Generative AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21095v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) grounds Large Language Model (LLM) outputs in external knowledge, but RAG systems usually trust whatever they retrieve, creating a Security-Reliability Gap: high semantic relevance does not guarantee factual truth. Adversaries exploit this through knowledge poisoning, inserting malicious documents to cause targeted misinformation. We propose an Evaluation Agent, middleware that combines Natural Language Inference (NLI) factual verification, a five-signal poison detector with relevance-weighted aggregation, and a Trust Index T = 0.4 F + 0.35 C + 0.25 (1 - P ) with a non-linear dampener for high-contamination contexts. On TruthfulQA with Llama 3.3 70B, the agent reaches 91% accuracy and 100% precision, with 100% recall on instruction injection, while in-place edits, such as entity swaps, remain hard to detect. Across three LLMs the Trust Index stays discriminative, with a Receiver Operating Characteristic Area Under the Curve (ROC-AUC) of 0.73 to 0.81; generation style matters more than model size, and per-LLM threshold calibration restores baseline competitive accuracy, whereas a weaker FEVER result shows that cross-dataset generalization requires domain-specific calibration. In a software-engineering use case, a secure-coding assistant over guidance from the Open Worldwide Application Security Project (OWASP) Top 10 and the Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE), the agent reliably blocks instruction injection of unsafe advice (F1 92%), while contradiction and subtle semantic weakening remain hard. Throughout, the agent measures detection of poisoned context before generation, not whether the LLM adopts the injected misinformation. We release the proposed approach, attack generator, and experimental artifacts at the link: https://github.com/GPT-Laboratory/TrustworthyRAG.</p></details> | <details><summary>7 pag...</summary><p>7 pages, 1 figure, 6 tables. Published in the Main Research Track of the Twenty-First International Conference on Software Engineering Advances (ICSEA 2026)</p></details> |
| **[Reliability Stress Testing of RAG Retrieval under Query-Relevant Corpus Poisoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04264v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>External text can affect a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system only after the retriever admits it to the model context. We stress this admission step while varying document content, attacker query information, per-target poison budget, and contamination scope. Across 623 Benchmarking Information Retrieval (BEIR) queries from SciFact and NFCorpus, five query-targeted documents per query reduce classical-retriever nDCG@10 by 40.2-61.5% in isolated-target stress. Full-query anchoring explains most Best Matching 25 (BM25) degradation, whereas appended text also shifts latent semantic analysis (LSA) and Hybrid rankings. A pretrained BGE-small validation reaches 100% target exposure for query-only P=5 on both datasets; adding the injection payload changes SciFact nDCG only from 0.269 to 0.271 and lowers NFCorpus exposure slightly to 98.8%, indicating that query anchoring remains the dominant retrieval-side factor for this dense model. Query length materially changes the interpretation of partial-information attacks: on 162 long SciFact queries, two random terms cover 19.9% of eligible terms and still yield 39.5% Hybrid target exposure. Multi-target contamination adds cross-target interference. Overlap-based admission can remove full-query poisons but may sharply reduce clean retrieval utility, and maximal marginal relevance (MMR) leaves exposure unchanged under the tested configuration. The study measures retrieval-layer failures, not downstream instruction following.</p></details> |  |
| **[Revisiting RAG Retrievers: An Information Theoretic Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.21553v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems rely critically on the retriever module to surface relevant context for large language models. Although numerous retrievers have recently been proposed, each built on different ranking principles such as lexical matching, dense embeddings, or graph citations, there remains a lack of systematic understanding of how these mechanisms differ and overlap. Existing benchmarks primarily compare entire RAG pipelines or rely on aggregate ranking metrics (Recall, MRR, nDCG) that assume item-level independence, providing little guidance on redundancy or complementarity between retrievers. This work presents MIGRASCOPE, a Mutual Information based RAG Retriever Analysis Scope. We introduce principled metrics grounded in information theory to quantify retrieval quality, redundancy/synergy, and marginal contribution. Although the framework is designed to be applicable to any setting where a ground-truth answer can construct a pseudo chunk distribution, the present empirical study focuses on multi-hop question answering - a demanding setting where retriever complementarity is especially pronounced. On four corpora and thirteen SOTA retrievers spanning lexical, dense, and graph-based families, we show that (i) the MI-based divergence metric captures retriever quality dimensions not reflected by Recall or MRR, (ii) graph-based retrievers are redundant with each other while BM25 remains informationally complementary, and (iii) in the multi-hop QA setting, a carefully chosen ensemble guided by MI-based attribution can outperform any single retriever. We position these findings as task-specific insights and provide a reusable framework for retriever analysis that practitioners can re-estimate on their own target domains.</p></details> |  |
| **[IAMRAGBENCH: Benchmarking Authorization Expansions in LLM-Generated AWS IAM Policies under RAG Poisoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03431v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) using retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can synthesize Amazon Web Services (AWS) Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies from tasks and operational documents. Attacker-controlled context may induce provider-valid policies that exceed trusted authorization requirements. We present IAMRAGBENCH, a 60-case benchmark spanning scope, conditions, explicit denies, trust, permissions boundaries, and role delegation. It combines deterministic authorization-envelope scoring with case-specific validation, including request-level checks where supported. An attack requires a provider-valid clean policy that satisfies the task and a provider-valid treatment policy that violates the envelope, matches a predeclared goal, preserves an intended request, and newly authorizes a forbidden request. Across five endpoints, included-context exposure produces 49 exact authorization deltas, including 46 goal-matched attacks (46/292; 15.75\%). End-to-end BM25 over 720 documents retrieves the designated poison for all 60 tasks at \(k=5\) but produces 7/294 goal-matched attacks (2.38\%). Presenting the same weakening as a final security architecture exception produces 74/116 attacks in a frozen two-endpoint stress test (63.79\%) and 115/294 in a post hoc all-five extension using the same frozen prompts (39.12\%). In a replay of 194 provider-valid treatment policies that violate task requirements, AWS \texttt{CheckNoNewAccess} reports no new access for four of 168 goal-matched exact attacks. The full pre-deployment rule rejects all 171 exact deltas and routes five unresolved cases to review. These findings support treating IAM copilots as untrusted proposal generators whose outputs require independent authorization checks before deployment.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 4 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[Bridging Static and Agentic RAG for Taiwanese Historical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23056v2)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) enables language models to adapt retrieval based on previously retrieved evidence, but it remains unclear whether such adaptive orchestration consistently outperforms well-designed static pipelines. We conduct a controlled comparison of agentic and static RAG for Taiwanese historical question answering, sharing the same generator and hybrid retrieval backend. Despite similar aggregate performance, the two pipelines differ on 70.83% of questions, with their advantages largely canceling out when averaged. An oracle that selects the better response per question improves the composite score by 0.2417 over the better individual pipeline, revealing substantial headroom for question-level selection. We therefore introduce a post-hoc selector that compares the two responses and their cited evidence, significantly outperforming either individual pipeline and recovering 60.34% of the oracle headroom. These results show that aggregate comparisons can obscure meaningful question-level differences between retrieval strategies, suggesting that exploiting their complementarity may be more fruitful than seeking a universally superior pipeline.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by TAAI 2026</p></details> |
| **[RAG-TRACE: Tracing Measurement Evidence and Diagnostic Completeness in AI-Generated ECG Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01409v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Purpose: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can introduce electrocardiogram (ECG) criteria that lack patient-specific measurement support. This study evaluates the reliability of retrieved diagnostic criteria in AI-generated ECG reports. Methods: We analyzed 1,083 human-validated PTB-XL patient records using a library of 47 criteria cards, including 33 without numeric verification rules. Language models generated structured reports with and without retrieval. Criterion applicability was evaluated using Marquette 12SL measurements, with independent assessment using Uni-G and comparison against expert-validated diagnostic labels. Results: Keyword retrieval introduced untestable or measurement-disconfirmed criteria in 46.8% of GPT-4.1-mini reports. A deterministic verification rule withdrew 647 criterion applications, including 639 without numeric checks, while preserving 268 of 269 applications independently supported by Uni-G. Restricting retrieval to 14 testable criteria reduced the initial unsupported-criterion rate to 13.8%. Under matched instructions and an identical output schema, retrieval increased unsupported-criterion reports from 17.4% to 31.1%, while hypertrophy recall remained unchanged at 62.6%. Conclusion: These findings identify a fundamental limitation of retrieval-based clinical report generation: retrieved criteria do not necessarily provide valid patient-specific evidence. Measurement-based verification can reduce unsupported criterion applications, but diagnostic validity requires independent assessment.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code and study materials available at https://github.com/Elyasirankhah/RAG-TRACE</p></details> |
| **[DeFT-RAG: Decomposing Multi-Hop Questions into Self-Contained Sub-Questions with Reusable Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37826v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) lets a large language model (LLM) answer questions by retrieving relevant passages from a large corpus. However, RAG struggles with multi-hop questions, which require chaining facts spread across several documents through intermediate entities that the question never mentions. Graph-based RAG methods retrieve once from the entities a question names and so rarely recover the full evidence chain. Iterative RAG methods recover it step by step, but their accuracy is limited by the many tasks they ask the LLM to perform, such as planning the sub-questions, deciding their dependencies, rewriting them as answers arrive, and keeping track of what is known. We propose DeFT-RAG, a RAG framework built on three components. First, DeFT-RAG generates one self-contained sub-question at a time from the original question and the sub-questions answered so far with their answers (QA pairs). Each sub-question names every entity it asks about, which removes the need to plan, track dependencies or rewrite, and lets the LLM focus on a single fine-grained task at a time. Second, DeFT-RAG moves the remaining LLM work outside the LLM: rule-based updates to a QA table that keeps track of what is known, and yes/no decisions made by a fine-tuned lightweight decision model. Third, DeFT-RAG caches the QA pairs and reuses an answer when a later question asks a sub-question with the same meaning. On MuSiQue, 2WikiMultiHopQA and HotpotQA, DeFT-RAG achieves the best accuracy among thirteen baselines with both gpt-4o-mini and Qwen3.6 as the backbone, while giving the LLM fewer input tokens than any other iterative method. With a QA cache shared across 1,000 MuSiQue questions, 23.1% of sub-questions are answered from the cache, which reduces LLM calls by 12.9% without lowering accuracy.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 2 figures, 7 tables. Code: https://github.com/Dingyi-Kang/DeFT-RAG</p></details> |

| **[RAG Strategies for Natural Language-Based SQL Query and REST API Call Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07086v3)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise software systems commonly expose functionality through relational databases and REST APIs, requiring users to understand schemas, endpoints, and parameters. We evaluate three training-free RAG configurations - Standard RAG, Relevance Filtering RAG, and Iterative Retrieval RAG - for SQL query generation, REST API call generation, and a combined task requiring routing between both operation types. Using SAP Transactional Banking, we constructed an expert-reviewed dataset with mock execution screening and compared the configurations under database-only, API-only, and mixed-documentation settings. Without retrieval, exact match accuracy is 0% across all tasks, whereas retrieval achieves mock execution success of up to 79.30% and component match accuracy of up to 78.86%. In the combined task, Iterative Retrieval RAG achieves higher exact match accuracy than Standard RAG (10.29% vs. 7.45%), with similar mock execution success (68.93% vs. 67.10%). However, for REST API call generation under mixed documentation, Iterative Retrieval RAG achieves lower mock execution success than Standard RAG (50.53% vs. 75.79%). Overall, retrieval-control strategies show task- and metric-dependent behavior under mixed-documentation settings.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in the IEEE Proceedings of the 3rd International Conference on Foundation and Large Language Models (FLLM 2026), Barcelona, Spain, November 17-20, 2026</p></details> |
| **[VisR-RAG-Fin: Benchmarking Multimodal RAG over Visually Rich Financial Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24822v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise documents such as financial reports are increasingly processed by automated systems, yet they are primarily designed for human reading and exhibit complex visual structure, combining dense text with tables, charts, diagrams, and rich layouts. In this paper, we present a comprehensive empirical study of multimodal RAG for page-level Question Answering (QA) over financial documents. We introduce VisR-RAG-Fin (Visually Rich RAG for Finance), an open-source benchmark constructed through a general pipeline for visually rich documents that preserves original layout and visual content. Using this benchmark, we systematically analyze retrieval and generation behavior under text-only, multimodal, and hybrid configurations, providing a foundation for rigorous evaluation of RAG systems on complex enterprise document collections. Overall, this work provides actionable insights for practitioners designing and evaluating multimodal RAG pipelines over complex, visually rich enterprise documents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the main research track of ACM SIGIR-AP 2026, Singapore, December 13-15, 2026</p></details> |
| **[RAGit: Turning Document Preprocessing into an Experimental Variable for RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24659v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Document preprocessing choices can substantially affect retrieval in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipelines, yet they are often selected heuristically and are difficult to compare under consistent conditions. RAGit represents parsing, chunking, indexing, retrieval, and evaluation as configurable experimental stages, enabling controlled comparisons while the remaining downstream settings are kept fixed. It preserves provenance from retrieved chunks to source pages and maps heterogeneous retrieval units to a common page-level ranking for evaluation. The current implementation supports multiple parsers and chunking strategies, dense and late-interaction retrieval, reusable intermediate artifacts, and multi-configuration benchmarking. We demonstrate RAGit through controlled experiments comparing alternative preprocessing configurations under a fixed retrieval setup, illustrating how preprocessing can be treated as an explicit experimental variable. RAGit and its documentation are publicly available on GitHub (https://github.com/OmarElbachyr/ragit). A demonstration video is available at https://youtu.be/tsst-Bhfz4I.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages, 2 figures, 4 tables. Accepted at the Tool Demonstration Track of the 33rd Asia-Pacific Software Engineering Conference (APSEC 2026)</p></details> |
| **[REAP: Enhancing RAG with Recursive Evaluation and Adaptive Planning for Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.09966v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has been extensively employed to mitigate hallucinations in large language models (LLMs). However, existing methods for multi-hop reasoning tasks often lack global planning, increasing the risk of falling into local reasoning impasses. Insufficient exploitation of retrieved content and the neglect of latent clues fail to ensure the accuracy of reasoning outcomes. To overcome these limitations, we propose Recursive Evaluation and Adaptive Planning (REAP), whose core idea is to explicitly maintain structured sub-tasks and facts related to the current task through the Sub-task Planner (SP) and Fact Extractor (FE) modules. SP maintains a global perspective, guiding the overall reasoning direction and evaluating the task state based on the outcomes of FE, enabling dynamic optimization of the task-solving trajectory. FE performs fine-grained analysis over retrieved content to extract reliable answers and clues. These two modules incrementally enrich a logically coherent representation of global knowledge, enhancing the reliability and the traceability of the reasoning process. Furthermore, we propose a unified task paradigm design that enables effective multi-task fine-tuning, significantly enhancing SP's performance on complex, data-scarce tasks. We conduct extensive experiments on multiple public multi-hop datasets, and the results demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms existing RAG methods in both in-domain and out-of-domain settings, validating its effectiveness in complex multi-hop reasoning tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>publi...</summary><p>published in AAAI 2026</p></details> |
| **[Upgrade the Retriever First: What an LLM Call per Chunk Buys in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23533v4)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many RAG pipelines pay one LLM call per chunk at indexing time to add titles, summaries, keywords and questions (enrichment) or document context (contextual retrieval, CR). We ask what that call buys over free structure and a better retriever. Our advice: upgrade the retriever first, chunk at headers, and enrich mainly for lexical retrieval. Under a plan made public before any index was built, 7B enrichment beats a free section-path prefix under a hybrid retriever by +5.5 recall points on Qasper; on FreshStack its gain (+2.7) does not survive Holm adjustment. It is equivalent to CR, which sends 5.8-8.7 times the input tokens, and Qasper answer F1 (7B reader) is equivalent for both contrasts. Header-led chunks beat 512-character windows; on FreshStack they lose to overlapping 256-token windows. Under qwen3-embedding and a reranker, the two strongest of eight retrievers (named as secondary before any outcome was computed), the CIs of 7B enrichment against the free prefix fall within the equivalence margins; under BM25 it gains +8.7. Exploratory analyses attribute the hybrid gain to document expansion and to repairing an untuned rank fusion; the free prefix under either strong retriever beats hybrid enrichment by +3.4 to +6.3 points, though the best 120B-indexed configuration leads the best free one by +2.5 on Qasper. | **[RIT-RAG: Navigating Document Corpora with Retrieval-Induced Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11370v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds language models in external corpora. Hence, they first commit to a single document using a document retriever and cannot recover from a wrong choice. We propose RIT-RAG (Retrieval-Induced Tree RAG), which combines content retrieval with structural navigation. Offline, RIT-RAG builds a tree for each document from its table of contents or sitemap. At query time, it retrieves a broad set of chunks and uses their positions to induce manageable sub-trees, potentially across multiple documents. An LLM agent navigate these sub-trees, selectively reads promising nodes, and reformulates queries when needed. Thus, retrieval proposes where to look, while the agent decides what to read. Across financial, scientific, and customer-support benchmarks, RIT-RAG achieves the highest answer accuracy among vanilla, graph-based, and agentic baselines. On EntQABench, our new benchmark of 2.84 million technical-documentation webpages, it improves accuracy by 6.6--11.4 points over the strongest baseline across three LLMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[FuseRAG: Fusing Structure and Evidence into Inference for Cost-Efficient Graph-based RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22818v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) exploits relationships among evidence to support multi-hop question answering. Our main idea is to separate reusable evidence content from query-aware structural interactions in latent space, while retaining only a few explicit facts as semantic anchors. The resulting latent representations are injected into decoder self-attention, where they reshape attention over evidence and thereby influence hidden states and next-token probabilities. This design preserves graph structure through latent interactions, enables evidence reuse beyond exact token-prefix matching. Experiments on three multi-hop question-answering datasets with Llama-3-8B and Qwen3.5-9B show that FuseRAG improves answer quality while achieving up to 2.7x and 2.2x TTFT speedups over the fastest graph-based baseline, respectively.</p></details> |  |
| **[Evidence-Gated Bilingual RAG for Reliable Consumer Banking Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22652v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Consumers often describe what they need rather than use the terminology found on a banking website. | **[RAG-RST: Retrieved Exemplars as In-Context Discourse Schema for RST Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22150v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In Rhetorical Structure Theory (RST), each edge of a discourse tree carries a nuclearity label, marking which span holds the central content, and a relation drawn from a closed inventory of functional categories. Predicting these labels therefore requires a formal discourse schema, and exposure to discourse-rich pretraining text does not supply it: given only a target EDU pair, a strong LLM recovers nuclearity at 0.794 F1 and relation at 0.738 F1. We propose RAG-RST, a retrieval-augmented framework that supplies this schema as in-context demonstration rather than through parameter updates, raising nuclearity to 0.846 F1 and relation to 0.788 F1 with no training. Two configurations are compared: a precomputed strategy that labels all EDU pairs before parsing, and a dynamic strategy that retrieves at each reduce step, so that the exemplars reaching the generator depend on the reductions the parser has already committed to. Both outperform existing RST parsers across span, nuclearity, relation, and full tree metrics, with the dynamic variant consistently strongest. To address the above limitation, we introduce a CE-RAG4EM (Cost-Efficient RAG for Entity Matching) framework that reduces overhead through blocking-based batch retrieval and generation. We investigate the benefits of this framework through a systematic analysis of RAG systems for entity matching, focusing on blocking-aware batch optimizations and retrieval granularity. Extensive experiments on nine widely used real-world entity matching benchmark datasets show that CE-RAG4EM under a zero-shot setting achieves comparable or improved matching quality while substantially reducing end-to-end overhead. The results demonstrate that our CE-RAG4EM closes the knowledge grounding gap of the LLM-EM baseline and yields significant improvements in F1 scores on datasets with ambiguous or numerically sparse attributes, while its batch retrieval and inference substantially reduce the overhead and maintain competitive matching quality compared to naive RAG4EM. Our analysis identifies clear performance-overhead trade-offs across blocking-based batch sizes and retrieval granularity, showing how matching quality and computational cost shift with each design choice. | **[Poisoning the Neighborhood: A Topology-Aware Topic-Level Attack on Large-Scale HNSW-based RAG Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21464v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) mitigates hallucinations and knowledge staleness in large language models using external knowledge. For generation, we propose an entity-claim promotion attack combining evidence anchoring and entity-claim coupling to promote the target entity via a target claim across different query regions. On 2M-document Wikipedia, our attack maintains over 72% retrieval success and reaches 31.29% attack success, substantially outperforming baselines; domain-specific experiments further support its effectiveness. Structural analyses show that the Hub-Satellite topology forms in HNSW, indicating that graph structure contributes to improved poisoned-document retrieval. We implement both as Loud and Clear (LoC), which combines higher-resolution pages with OCR from query-relevant regions without ground-truth annotations or additional training. Crucially, evidence recovery does not produce a proportional reduction in total error: premature resolutions and abstentions increase as missing-evidence failures decrease. | **[When More Documents Hurt RAG: Measuring and Reducing Vector Search Dilution with Domain-Scoped Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11350v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) degrades when applied to large, heterogeneous document collections. Furthermore, we can discover these scopes without hand-curated metadata. Based on these varied outcomes, our practical recommendation is simple: soft-scope first, then perform a single synthesis call.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 5 figures, 20 tables. | **[Copying Is Not Grounding: Training Faithful Long-Form RAG with Authored-Sentence Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18442v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In search engines and assistants, long-form RAG answers can mislead readers with claims their sources do not support. Faithfulness rewards, the share of supported sentences, train models toward fewer such claims. We show that these rewards are hacked by copying. A sentence copied from the passages is supported almost by construction, so trained models learn to copy rather than ground what they write. Common defenses only move the hack: a KL or copy penalty leaves copying in place or trades it for shorter, emptier answers and refusals. GRPO, the standard RL algorithm, makes it worse by giving every sentence its answer's advantage, reinforcing copied and grounded sentences alike. We propose Authored-Sentence Credit (ASC) , which fixes both by moving GRPO's reward and advantage from the answer to the sentence level. The reward credits only sentences that are authored rather than copied, say more than the question, and are supported by the passages. The advantage compares each sentence with every sentence sampled for the question, so one that earns no credit ranks last and is never reinforced. Across four models and two in-domain, three held-out corpora, only ASC lowers the error rate of authored sentences and writes more supported ones in every setting, by 38% and 46% at the median under held-out evaluators, giving readers more grounded information. It takes none of these shortcuts, and two LLM judges comparing whole answers prefer no baseline to the base model but rate ASC's answers on par with it. We conclude that a faithfulness reward should credit what a model writes, not what it copies.</p></details> |  |
| **[PerCache: Predictive Hierarchical Cache for RAG Applications on Mobile Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.11553v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has become a de facto paradigm in various large language model (LLM)-driven applications on mobile devices, such as mobile assistants leveraging personal emails or meeting records. However, due to lengthy prompts and resource constraints, mobile RAG systems exhibit significantly high response latency. To address this issue, one promising approach is to reuse intermediate computational results across different queries to eliminate redundant computation. But most existing approaches, such as key-value (KV) cache reuse and semantic cache reuse, are designed for cloud settings and perform poorly on mobile devices as they overlook the distinctive characteristics of mobile RAG. We propose PerCache, a novel hierarchical cache framework designed for reducing end-to-end latency of personalized RAG applications on mobile platforms. PerCache adopts a hierarchical architecture that progressively matches similar queries and query-key-value (QKV) cache to maximize the reuse of intermediate results at different computing stages. To improve cache hit rate, PerCache applies a predictive method to populate the cache with queries that are likely to be raised in the future. In addition, PerCache can adapt its configurations to dynamic system loads, aiming to maximize caching utility with constrained resource consumption. We implement PerCache on top of an existing mobile LLM inference engine and deploy it on commodity mobile phones. Extensive evaluations show that PerCache achieves the lowest average end-to-end latency across five datasets and two LLM architectures, reducing latency by up to 63.5% over the strongest baseline while maintaining comparable answer quality.</p></details> |  |
| **[CacheSmith: Accurate RAG Serving under Latency SLOs with Adaptive KV Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18925v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents CacheSmith, a KV cache reuse system for RAG prefill that maximizes quality-gated SLO attainment. While context caching lowers prefill latency by reusing precomputed KV states for retrieved chunks, it misses cross-chunk attention that a full prefill would compute over concatenated inputs. On four benchmarks across varying arrival rates and burstiness levels, CacheSmith improves quality-gated SLO attainment by up to 27.5%, over four state-of-the-art baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[EvoRAG: Making Knowledge Graph-based RAG Automatically Evolve through Feedback Backpropagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15676v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Knowledge Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (KG-RAG) has emerged as a promising paradigm for enhancing LLM reasoning by retrieving multi-hop paths from KGs. However, existing KG-RAG frameworks often underperform in real-world scenarios because the pre-captured knowledge dependencies are not tailored to the downstream task or its evolving requirements. These frameworks struggle to adapt to task-specific requirements and lack mechanisms to filter low-contribution knowledge during generation. We observe that feedback on generated responses offers effective supervision for improving KG quality, as it directly reflects user expectations and provides insights into the correctness and usefulness of the output. However, a key challenge lies in effectively linking response-level feedback to triplet-level contribution evaluation and knowledge updates in the KG. In this work, we propose EvoRAG, a self-evolving KG-RAG framework that leverages the feedback over generated responses to continuously refine the KG and enhance reasoning accuracy. EvoRAG introduces a feedback-driven backpropagation mechanism that attributes feedback to retrieved paths by measuring their utility for response and propagates this utility back to individual triplets, supporting fine-grained KG refinements towards more adaptive and accurate reasoning. Through EvoRAG, we establish a closed loop that couples feedback, LLM, and graph data, continuously enhancing the performance and robustness in real-world scenarios. Experimental results show that EvoRAG improves reasoning accuracy by $7.34\%$ over state-of-the-art KG-RAG frameworks. The source code has been made available at https://github.com/iDC-NEU/EvoRAG.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ICDE 2027</p></details> |
| **[Fast and Accurate: Improving Personalized LLM Performance by Semantic Concentration Inspired Workload Split between RAG and Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15921v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With ever increasing personalized large language model (LLM) services, the next question to answer is how to improve the quality of services/users' experiences, which is indicated by personalized LLM generation accuracy/quality and inference latency. Briefly, PUR partitions user's personal historical data and assigns semantic concentrated ``core'' information to PEFT for offline parametric fine-tuning, while retaining dispersed information for RAG. During the online LLM inference, PUR directly generates responses for ``core'' queries covered by the PEFT region and invokes RAG only for sporadic ``peripheral'' queries. Experimental results show that, by appropriately splitting workload between PEFT and RAG, PUR consistently achieves the best generation accuracy/quality for various users across multiple tasks, while reducing inference latency by 14.4%--46.6% compared with the best performed method.</p></details> |  |
| **[OntoAR-HAR: Ontology-Guided Agentic RAG with Personalized Memory for Human Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15292v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs increasingly support sensor-based human activity recognition (HAR) by linking motion signals with activity semantics and physical knowledge. However, existing LLM/RAG-based HAR typically searches single-level activity spaces using static population knowledge. Consequently, they compare many unrelated candidates using the same static population evidence and overlook personalized motion patterns. We propose OntoAR-HAR, an ontology-guided agentic RAG framework combining static population and personalized knowledge. Its tree-structured activity ontology decomposes classification into local decisions among semantically related activities. The static store supplies node-constrained examples, while offline-discovered executable signal features provide numerical evidence for local distinctions. During inference, the personalized store collects selected user observations, which the LLM integrates with static knowledge and signal features while traversing the ontology to an activity label. Cross-subject and heterogeneous-sensing experiments show complementary gains from hierarchical retrieval and personalized memory across multiple LLM backbones, achieving state-of-the-art performance. By explicitly modeling hierarchical relations across activity granularities, OntoAR-HAR also addresses the long-overlooked semantic inclusion problem and avoids treating related labels as independent categories.</p></details> |  |
| **[Retrieving Complementary Evidence for Multi-Hop RAG with Semantic Gain Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15285v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-hop retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) requires finding documents whose evidence is useful in combination. Analyses of supporting documents and improvements in three iterative retrieval frameworks further support its effectiveness as a reusable retrieval component.</p></details> |  |
| **[SAFE-KV: Causal Suffix Repair for Behavior-Preserving Non-Prefix KV Cache Reuse in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15186v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) reuses the same documents across many requests, making non-prefix KV-cache reuse attractive. We formulate non-prefix KV reuse as a behavior-preservation problem with full prefill on the exact prompt as the reference, and introduce SAFE-KV, which repairs a composed cache by re-encoding the causal suffix from a selected start document. On Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, prefix-conditioned composition preserves only 47.2% of HotpotQA-500 and 37.8% of 2Wiki-500 full-prefill answers; SAFE-KV with risk-guided K2 selection restores 99.8%/98.4% (donor-copy protocol) and 100.0%/98.4% with actual suffix forwarding. Document-only and fixed-window repairs recover far less, because the document states after the repaired region keep histories that omit it. Reuse without repair is exact up to four documents and degrades at sixteen, where suffix repair recovers 98-100%. Faithful repair re-encodes most of the context, so in a single-request L40S study SAFE-KV's operating rule serves every workload by full prefill. Code: https://github.com/XingjianZhang-dev/safe-kv</p></details> |
| **[Post-Generation Verification Dominates Retrieval Optimization: A 2^4 Factorial Ablation of RAG Pipeline Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35774v3)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern RAG pipelines stack many enhancement features, but these features are typically validated in isolation, leaving their interactions unmeasured. The design crosses 16 configurations, 24 queries spanning eight interaction types, and two cloud-class models (768 designed runs, 759 completed and scored) on five public documents (78-492 pages), and every answer is scored against a verified reference. On this corpus and task, post-generation verification dominates: CC is the strongest feature (d=+0.48, p<0.001), improving accuracy, completeness, and usefulness simultaneously, and CC alone (4.31/5) is ahead of every configuration without it, including the three-feature SE+AS+ToC (4.11). These main effects hold when every response is re-scored by two alternate LLM judges. The highest-quality configuration roughly doubles baseline latency, producing a genuine quality-latency Pareto frontier of six configurations. Feature utility is strongly query-type dependent (CC's completeness effect reaches d=+0.83 on completeness-demanding queries), so single-query-type evaluations systematically mis-rank features. | **[Training-Free BioNEL-RAG: Advancing Biomedical Entity Linking through Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14789v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biomedical Named Entity Linking (BioNEL) is the task of mapping mentions extracted from biomedical texts to canonical entities or concepts in the standardized knowledge base. | **[Frustratingly Easy Agentic RAG via Portable Skills: A Case Study in Customer Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14428v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise workflow automation typically requires retrieving a complete and accurate set of relevant information from large operational document corpora (SOPs, policy documents) given a specific scenario or request. We report a deployment case study of a simpler alternative: a portable skill consisting of a natural-language instruction, lightweight tools (a retrieval API, format-checking scripts), and a corpus enriched with an LLM-generated navigation layer, which is a structured "Wikipedia-style" index over the underlying documents. This agentic RAG approach is straightforward to implement (1-2 engineer days), portable across capable agentic harnesses (e.g., Claude Code), and performs strongly on the challenging task of CS resolution workflow generation, achieving 99.2% procedural equivalence to human-approved ground truth and 83.3% evidence sufficiency. Our claim is scoped to that setting: a mature, well-curated corpus, a latency-tolerant workflow, and human review of every case. | **[ORCAGen: Orchestrating Context-Aware Malware Deception with RAG-Guided Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12415v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Malware defenses often remove or isolate suspicious programs as quickly as possible. While effective for containment, this approach can also waste an opportunity to observe attacker behavior and deploy targeted countermeasures. ORCAGen takes a different approach: it uses GenAI to build malware-specific deception playbooks offline, validates them before deployment, and enforces only the verified logic at runtime. ORCAGen combines Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) with structured prompt engineering to generate both proof-of-concept (PoC) malware and corresponding deception orchestration code. A curated knowledge base (KB) of malware procedures and active defense strategies grounds the generation process, helping the LLM produce threat-specific and executable deception logic rather than generic or hallucinated outputs. We evaluate ORCAGen across GPT-4o, GPT-5.5, Gemini 3.5 Flash, Qwen3-Coder, and Claude Sonnet 4.5 using execution success, hallucination rate, refinement effort, deception effectiveness, and runtime overhead. Across the evaluated scenarios, GPT-5.5 required the fewest refinements and produced no observed hallucinated APIs, while Gemini 3.5 Flash achieved the lowest response time and runtime overhead. GNN-based retrievers achieve higher graph coverage with an efficient training setup, whereas the vector-search baseline excels at single-hop datasets.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to AACL-IJCNLP 2026, 9 pages, 19 figures</p></details> |
| **[RAG-Stress: Probing the Limits of Evidence Reliance in Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11183v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Following retrieved evidence does not guarantee factual correctness: misleading evidence can induce a model to replace an answer it previously gave correctly. The protocol holds the question and reference answer fixed, edits one assertion to support a designated incorrect answer, and crosses two source priority policies with three positions of the answer span within the evidence text. We measure misleading rate (MR) on each model's subset of questions answered correctly without retrieval, alongside clean accuracy on the full evaluation set. We evaluate fifteen systems spanning API models, open models, and search agents trained with reinforcement learning on TriviaQA-RC, HotpotQA, and SearchQA, with additional English and Chinese MedQA evaluations. Instructions that prioritize documents consistently produce higher MR than those permitting reliance on prior knowledge. Averaged over models and positions, the gap ranges from 10.9 to 13.5 percentage points across the three QA datasets. Mean MR follows End $>$ Beginning $>$ Middle under both policies, although individual models do not uniformly follow this ordering. A separate paired audit of 500 questions and two checkpoints supports increased harmful override without establishing a corresponding improvement in beneficial correction. These findings distinguish evidence adherence from factual reliability and motivate evaluating whether retrieved evidence preserves, replaces, or corrects a model's answers.</p></details> |  |

| **[EC-RAG: Event Chain Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08674v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current large video-language models (LVLMs) still face challenges when dealing with long videos, mainly because frames are often processed independently, making it difficult to capture temporal dependencies across events. Instead of retrieving isolated frames or text segments, EC-RAG first partitions the video into semantically coherent segments, represents each segment using multi-modal signals, and then links them into a structured chain that preserves temporal order and captures inter-event relationships. Given a query, the system identifies relevant events within this chain and gathers supporting evidence from the associated modalities. Our approach offers several practical advantages: (i) event-level abstraction that better reflects how video content is naturally structured, enabling more reliable localization compared to frame-level retrieval; (ii) structured multi-modal fusion that aggregates speech, text, and visual cues at the event level, allowing complementary information to be more effectively utilized during reasoning; and (iii) plug-and-play compatibility with existing LVLM backbones, requiring no additional training or reliance on proprietary models. Code: https://github.com/Agentic-Systems-Lab/Agentic-AutoRAG</p></details> |
| **[Tool-calling retrieval versus vector RAG for a small Greek--English knowledge base: accuracy and robustness to how users type Greek](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08205v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Assistants grounded in a small, frequently edited knowledge base can retrieve through tool calls to a live data interface or through vector retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). We compare the two on KyGround, a benchmark of 198 questions drawn from the published records of a Greek--English agricultural platform on Kythera, Greece, with answers verified automatically against the records and each question posed in up to nine forms, including Greek without accents, in capitals and in three Latin-script (Greeklish) schemes. Its literal searches returned nothing when the router's arguments did not occur verbatim in a record, for example when it transliterated Greek into Latin script or combined words that occur in a record but not as one phrase, and the agent then abstained. | **[DrugMCTS: a drug repurposing framework combining multi-agent, RAG and Monte Carlo Tree Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.07426v4)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in large language models have demonstrated considerable potential in scientific domains such as drug repositioning. However, their effectiveness remains constrained when reasoning extends beyond the knowledge acquired during pretraining. Conventional approaches, such as fine-tuning or retrieval-augmented generation, face limitations in either imposing high computational overhead or failing to fully exploit structured scientific data. | **[Rethinking Knowledge Retrieval for Generation: A Survey on RAG Architectures and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01936v2)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable fluency and versatility across natural language tasks but remain fundamentally limited by their static knowledge and susceptibility to hallucinations, especially in domains requiring up to date or attribute grounded information. Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) addresses these challenges by integrating external retrieval mechanisms with generative models, enabling dynamic, context aware generation grounded in verifiable data sources. Anchored around four emerging axes efficiency, security, user centric interactivity, and complex reasoning we categorize recent innovations and highlight their implications for scalability, robustness, and personalization. The paper also reviews advances in evaluation protocols, domain specific applications, and architectural variants such as Naïve RAG, Advanced RAG, and Modular RAG. Finally, we identify persistent challenges and outline future directions aimed at advancing the integration of retrieval with LLMs for more grounded, interpretable, and controllable generation.</p></details> |  |

| **[Reframe: Observation-Aligned Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation for Agentic Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12886v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) and multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) offer complementary routes to post-training. For visual agents, however, tool use presents two related challenges. Models may overlook useful tool-returned observations, while poorly grounded actions can limit the evidence available for subsequent distillation. We introduce Reframe, a post-training framework that treats the observation state, rather than only the output distribution, as an object of alignment. First, Ground-Drop-GRPO (GD-GRPO) trains spatial and temporal specialists with evidence-aware rewards and stochastic removal of the original visual input, encouraging them to acquire grounded evidence and use the returned observations. Second, Observation-Gated-MOPD (OG-MOPD) replaces deficient tool actions with canonical ones mid-rollout and resumes distillation from the repaired observation. The two stages complement each other, as learning to rely on tool-returned observations can help the policy benefit from observation repair. Neither stage requires a task-specific SFT cold start. Extensive experiments over 14 image and video benchmarks demonstrate that Reframe outperforms both Mix-RL and vanilla MOPD. Ablation results show that GD-GRPO increases observation dependence and tool gain compared with outcome-based GRPO.</p></details> |  |
| **[World In-Context Learning for Agentic Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12885v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Trained action policies write manipulation skill into network weights, tying what is learned to the distribution it is learned from. In-context learning (ICL) avoids this coupling but has not matched trained policies, because a demonstration recorded in another scene carries no feedback from the world the robot currently faces. We observe that what an agent learns in context is semantic, recording task steps and object roles rather than visual appearance, so experience gathered in one world transfers to another. Based on this insight, we propose World In-Context Learning (WICL), a paradigm in which a frozen model starts from a demonstration, rehearses the task in a world such as a simulator, and writes the feedback into its context rather than its weights. We build RoboScript, a modular infrastructure that implements WICL for agentic manipulation. From a single demonstration, RoboScript learns each task through rehearsal in simulation, freezes the learned knowledge as a Task Memory, and deploys the frozen memory on new scenes with data isolation that ensures every deployment input is available on a real robot. On all 50 tasks of RoboTwin 2.0, RoboScript reaches 83.4% mean success across clean and randomized settings with one demonstration per task and no gradient updates, exceeding the strongest trained action policy by over 20 points. The method generalizes across visual distributions, improves through reflection, scales with the foundation model, and transfers to a physical dual-arm robot through cross-embodiment memory adaptation. With a world in the loop, a robot can learn a manipulation task in its context as well as it learns one in its weights. All code is available at https://github.com/ArcheOne-AI/RoboScript</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://ArcheOne-AI.github.io/RoboScript</p></details> |
| **[HiVe-Mem: Interleaving Horizontal Memory Expansion and Vertical Evidence Composition for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12872v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As multimodal agents accumulate increasingly long interaction histories, effectively retrieving and utilizing past information becomes critical for long-term interaction. Existing memory systems typically rely on semantic retrieval over compressed memories and expose predefined representations, which may miss relevant memories and overlook heterogeneous evidence needs across queries. We introduce \textbf{\hivemem}, a multimodal memory framework that interleaves \textbf{\textsc{H}}or\textbf{\textsc{i}}zontal Memory Expansion with \textbf{\textsc{Ve}}rtical Evidence Composition. | **[AutoBuddy: Evaluating Proactive, Always-On Agents on Task Discovery and Execution in a Simulated Workplace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12393v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLM agents become more autonomous, they are increasingly expected to run as always-on assistants on a user's behalf, identifying work to be done and completing it without being asked. It models an organisation's employees, projects, business systems and event history as a temporal graph, renders it into realistic workspace artefacts, derives ground-truth tasks from the events that create obligations, and computes each task's outcome from the world state; execution is judged by the agent's changes to the workspace. | **[Detecting Shadow AI: Unsanctioned LLM Agents on Enterprise Endpoints: A Taxonomy, an Open Detector and a Controlled Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12377v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Shadow IT, the use of technology that the IT organisation has not approved, is a long-standing governance problem. On a controlled bench of 12 synthetic agents, 3 AI components and 9 benign workloads, repeated 5 times on one workstation, two behavioural rules flagged every behavioural twin, while Agent Detector flagged none and detected every agent in the majority of runs. | **[Physics-Informed Multi-Agent Coordination for Hospital Patient Flow Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37022v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Efficient patient flow coordination across autonomous hospital departments is critical for mitigating overcrowding and balancing resource utilization. In this paper, we present a Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) framework titled \emph{Physics-Informed Multi-Agent Coordination}, which embeds empirically calibrated BCMP queueing topologies as physical priors within a decentralized multi-agent reinforcement learning architecture. Formulated as a Decentralized Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (Dec-POMDP) under coupled resource constraints, our method enables autonomous departmental agents to cooperatively negotiate patient routing and dynamic service scaling.
